r. O'Brien called the Czech Embassy so that it
mi ht be informed that Mir. Zamara was cleared by SCAP
to enter Japan between November 16, 1947 and November 30,
1947 for a stay of 21 days commencing the date of entry.
Mr. O'Brien noted from a duplicate of Mr. Zamara's appli-
cation for permission to enter Japan that Mr. Zamara
was presently residing at Bangkok and requested infor-
mation as to whether the Czech Government maintained
a diplomatic or consular establishment at Bangkok or
whether the Embassy would authorize Mr. O'Brien to send
a telegram charged to the Embassy to the American rep-
resentative at Bangkok so that Mr. zamara might be
documented.
Mr. O'Brien was informed by the officer of the
Czech Embassy with whom he spoke that the Czech Govern-
ment maintained no establishment of any kind at Bangkok
and requested that a cablegram charged to the Czech
GS

for Mr. Zamara. A note confirming this conversation

Reference the underlying memorandum of September 2, 1947 from
the ilitary Permit Section, War Department, indicating that the
following persons may proceed to Japan:
Very Reverend Mother Cristina Estrada
l'other Vonserrat Barjau
As Mr. Mohler, National Catholic Welfare Conference had pre-
viously inquired on these cases and requested to be informed when
the clearances had been received, Miss Wolberg telephoned him on
September 8, 1947. He informed her that there had been a mixup in
this case and that the Very Reverend Mother Cristina Estrada is
going to Janan, the order to which she belongs, and which has been
requesting her military ermit, does not know who Mother Monserrat
Earjau is. Instead of the latter they intend to send one Mother
FW.894.111
Henrietta C. M. Martindale, a British national, to Japan. Mr. Mohler
stated that the crder had sent a cable to Tokyo requesting the
application for permission for Mother Henrietta C. M. Martindale
to enter Japan and inquiring who Mother Monserrat Barjau is.
He said that an answer to this cable should be received this week
and asked that Miss iolberg hold up action on the memorandum from
the Military Permit Section until the matter was straightened out.
He said that he would inform Miss Wolberg as soon as the answer was
received from Tokyo. Wiss Wolberg said that no action would be taken
until she heard from Mr. Mohler.

Dear Mr. Hibbard:
Reference is made to Department of State letters
SPD 702.3394/7-1547 and DS 702.3394/7-1547.
Clearance has been received for Mr. Carlos Hounie
Fleurquin of Uruguay to enter Japan as authorized trade
representative of the Uruguayan Government with the under-
standing that logistic support initially will be furnished
by the occupation forces on a revenue basis.
It is assumed that Mr. Fleurquin will not perform
consular or diplomatic functions. If this is an incorrect
assumption, please clarify his status so that the theater
may be properly informed.
When Mr. Fleurquin is able to make independent arrange
ments for own facilities, consideration will be given to a
new application for clearance of Mrs. Fleurquin.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF PROTECTIVE SERVICES
SPEXXNAXXDIXISION
September 10, 1947
MEMORANDUL FOR THE FILES
Reference the underlying note of September 3, 1947 from the
Legation of Lebanon requesting permission for Mr. "Cherif Soubra"
to enter Japan. According to the note Mr. Soubra has been appointed
by the Lebanese Linistry of National Economy to go to Japan in
order to study the possibilities of trade between Lebanon and Japan.
It will be noted from the attached file that the Legation previously
approached the Department with a request for permission for lir. Sou-
bra to go to Japan. (The previous correspondence refers to Mr.
Soubra as "Sherif Abdulbadie Soubra". Kiss Wolberg telephoned Mrs. Ward
of the Lebanese Legation to inquire whether "Sherif Abdulbadie" and
"Cherif" are one and the same individual. Mrs. Ward said they are one
and the same and indicated that she believed Mr. Soubra is presently
in Lebanon.) A memorandum dated May 28, 1947 from the Wilitary Per-
mit Secreteriat informed the Department that the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers had not iven favorable consideration to
Mr. Soubra's application. This information was transmitted to the
Legation on June 23, 1947.
Upon receipt of the note of September 3, Miss Wolberg teleptoned
Captain Kecugh, kilitary Permit Section, War Department, to inquire
whether another application should be submitted or whether a letter
from this Department setting forth the contents of the new Lebanese
note would be sufficient as most of the information called for in the
application is already in the Military Permit Section's files on
Mr. Soubra. Captain Keough replied that he thought a letter from the
Department would be sufficient, and that if any additional information
were needed, he would let us know.
Miss Wolberg then telephoned Mr. Paige, JK to inform him of the
contents of the Lebanese note of September 3, 1947 and to clear with
him a draft letter to Colonel Lynch, Military Permit Section, War Depart-
C
ment requesting the necessary permission for Mr. Soubra. There was a
discussion as to whether the Lebanese intended Mr. Soubra to go as an
F
official representatives of his Government or in a private capacity.
ir. Paige indicated that when he had acknowledged by 'phone the Lega-
tion's note of August 11, 1947, in which it is stated that the Lebanese
Government is interested in sending "comnercial representatives" to
Japan, it was understood both by the Lebanese and by Mr. Paige that the
note referred to the desire of the Lebanese Government to have private
Lebanese businessmen go to Japan under the Lebanese quota. It was
understood, he said, that the Lebanese Government was not requesting
permission to send delegates there as officials of the Lebanese Government.
DS:MWo1berg:MMcC

September 8, 1947
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
Reference the underlying memorandum of August 29, 1947 from
the Military Permit Section, War Department, stating that Mr. Teofilo
Homsany Missri may enter Japan between September 1 and September 16,
1947, inclusive, for a three week stay. Upon receiving the afore-
mentioned memorandum, Miss Wolberg telephoned Mr. Chevalier, Conseler
of the Panemanian Embassy, to inform him of the sense of i: It was
agreed that there would hardly be time for the Embassy to transmit
this information to the Panamanian Foreign Office and for Mr. Missri
to get tc Japan by September 16. Consequently, Mr. Chevalier re-
quested that the Department endeavor to obtain permission for
Mr. Missri to enter Japan between October 1 and October 15, 1947.
Miss Wolberg said that an endeavor would be made to have this done and
that meanwhile a communication would be sent to the Embassy confirm-
ing this conversation and containing certain other information on
this case which has been received from the military authorities.
Kr. Chevalier requested that the original and two flimsies of the
communication be sent to the Embassy. Miss Wolberg then telephoned
Captain Keough, Military Permit Section, War Department, and informed
him of the situation d scribed above. Captain Keough said that he
would see to it that a cable was sent to the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers today requesting permission for Mr. Missri to enter
Japan between October 1 and October 15, 1947. He said that he would
inform Miss Wolberg as soon as a reply was received.

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
Form AD-1
Alien Departure Permit
Application No.
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ALIEN'S APPLICATION FOR PERMISSION TO DEPART FROM
THE UNITED STATES
(For use by all aliens who desire to depart from the United States)
No fee prescribed
Send no money
This application must be executed in quintuplicate and sworn to before an officer
authorized to administer oaths. Each blank space must be filled in. If the space provided
is not sufficient to include the information requested, additional sheets of paper may be
utilized, with a reference to the question answered.
Five unmounted photographs of the applicant, recently made, not smaller than 2 by 2
nor larger than 3 by 3 inches, must be furnished with this application; one photograph to
be firmly affixed to each copy of the application in the blank space provided therefor. Each
photograph must be signed by the applicant in such manner as not to obscure the features.
An applicant registered or enrolled, or subject to registration or enrollment, for mili-
tary service in the United States must submit with this application a statement from the
Secretary of War or from his local draft board stating whether there are any objections to
his departure from the United States.
Other evidence may be required by the Secretary of State concerning the facts in the
applicant's case before a conchusion is reached on the application.
The receipt of ihe application will be acknowledged, and the applicant will be notified
later concerning the decision reached upon the application. If the permit to depart is
granted it will be forwarded to the appropriate authorities at the port through which the
applicant intends to depart from the United States, and the application number will appear
on a notification card which will be sent to the applicant. By calling upon the port author-
ities prior to or at the time of departure and presenting the notification card and identify-
ing himself as the person to whom permission to depart has been granted by the Secretary
of State, the applicant may be permitted to depart from the United States through the port
specified.
This application should be filed at least 30 days before the applicant intends to depart.
Any permit issued upon the basis of this application is subject to revocation at any time.
Every question must be answered in some manner, as no application will be acted upon
until complete information is furnished and unless it appears that the applicant has been
granted permission to enter the country of his destination and to pass through all countries
en route.

Telegram Mo. Z-49375 of June 5, 1947 from CINCFE,
SUBJECT:
Tokyo to War regarding permission for seven
missionaries to enter Japan.
PARTICIPANTS:
Mrs. Flickinger, Military Permit Seetion,
Civil Affairs Division, War Department,
Mise Wolberg, SPD.
COPIES TO:
1—1493
Upon receiving a memorandum dated July 7, 1947 from
Dublin regarding the case of the Reverend Joseph Francis
O'Brien, Miss Wolberg called Mrs. Flickinger to ask whether
the Military Permit Section had any knowledge of the above-
mentioned telegram. After searching her files Mrs.Fliekinger
1ndicated that the telegram had been received in her Seetion
but that they had not yet been able to give the information
to the State Department. She indicated that there would
be no objeetion to our informing the interested persons
that they had been granted permission to enter Japan, with-
out walting for a communication from the Military Permit
Section. She also stated, however, that a memorandum re-
garding the seven persons mentioned above would be forth-
coming from the Military Permit Section.
In ansver to her inquiry, Miss Wolberg was informed
that there was no time limit on the validity of the per-
mission granted to the persons mentioned in the cable under
reference. Mrs. Flickinger stated that when the letter
mentloned in the last sentence of the cable was received,
SPD would be informed.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
SRECIXXODOXISTONK
DIVISION OF DIVISION OF PROTECTIVE SERVICES
PROTECTIVE SERVICES
Flle
SEP 23,1947
September 23, 1947.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
SUBJECT: Exit Permit Case of Jean L. Ashman.
Mr. Williams checked with Mr. Weymueller, American
President Lines agent in Washington, regarding the Ashman
case as outlined in George Ashman's letter of August 11,
1947. It was ascertained (1) that no exit or military
permit is required for a British national traveling to
Bombay from San Francisco, even though the vessel involved
stops at Japan, if the individual does not propose to dis-
embark at the Japanese port; (2) that the passenger l1st
of the MARINE LEOPARD which actually sailed from San
Francisco on September 5 carried the names of Mr. and Mrs.
George L. Ashman.
Presumably, therefore, the entire matter was straightened
out at San Francisco and the Ashmans were able to sail despite
their earlier difficulties with the Immigration Service.
Mr. Williams then checked with Mr. Horner of the INS
in Philadelphia to ascertain whether a reentry permit had
been 1ssued to Mrs. Ashman before her departure from the
United States. He described the circumstances and Mr.
Horner agreed that there was no reason why the permit should
not have been issued. Mr. Horner said he would look into
the matter and that (a) if the permit had been issued no
further action would be necessary; (b) if the permit had not
been 1ssued but the application was on file at Philadelphia
he would issue the permit and send it to Mrs. Ashman via the
American Consulate at Calcutta; (c) if no permit had been
issued and no application received, he would consult again
with Mr. Williams with a view to clearing the matter up. It
was agreed that if Mra Horner did not call Mr. Williams on
the case within the next/days the whole matter could be
regarded as closed and the papers sent to file without action
by the State Department.
DS:EwLIliams III:erk

HELPERS OF THE HOLY SOULS
112 EAST B6TH STREET
NEW YORK 2B, N. Y.
Sept. 5, 1947.
Exit Permit Unit,
U. S. Department of State,
Washington, D.C.
Dear Sirs:
Sister Marie de Ste. Oesarine (Lucie de
Nervo) the companion of Very Reverend Mother
St. Radegonde (Marguerite Marie de Courthille)
Superior General of the Society of the Helpers
of the Holy Souls, hopes to visil with the latter
our mission houses in Tokyo, Hiroshima, Yawata
in Japan; the two last mentioned having been
completely destroyed by bombing. About two
months stay would be needed in Japan, after which
two or three months more might be required for
the visit of our mission in Shanghai, China.
All our houses in the Orient have suffered
much because of the war, and the Religious there
have great need of the counsel and help of their
Superiors.
The Provincial House of the Society in the
United States is here at 112 E. 86 St. New York
City, and gladly assumes the responsibility of
supporting the two visitors in Japan and China,
and of aiding them in any contingency that may
arise.
Yours very truly,
Mary of O Ccririr Drovincial
Maric de sto Lisurine

Form AD-1
Alien Departure Permit
Application No.
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ALIEN'S APPLICATION FOR PERMISSION TO DEPART FROM
THE UNITED STATES
(For use by all aliens who desire to depart from the United States)
No fee prescribed
Send no money
This application must be executed in quintuplicate and sworn to before an officer
authorized to administer oaths. Each blank space must be filled in. If the space provided
is not sufficient to include the information requested, additional sheets of paper may be
utilized, with a reference to the question answered.
Five unmounted photographs of the applicant, recently made, not smaller than 2 by 2
nor larger than 3 by 3 inches, must be furnished with this application; one photograph to
be firmly affixed to each copy of the application in the blank space provided therefor. Each
photograph must be signed by the applicant in such manner as not to obscure the features.
An applicant registered or enrolled, or subject to registration or enrollment, for mili-
tary service in the United States must submit with this application a statement from the
Secretary of War or from his local draft board stating whether there are any objections to
his departure from the United States.
Other evidence may be required by the Secretary of State concerning the facts in the
applicant's case before a conclusion is reached on the application.
The receipt of the application will be acknowledged, and the applicant will be notified
later concerning the decision reached upon the application. If the permit to depart is
granted it will be forwarded to the appropriate authorities at the port through which the
applicant intends to depart from the United States, and the application number will appear
on a notification card which will be sent to the applicant. By calling upon the port author-
ities prior to or at the time of departure and presenting the notification card and identify-
ing himself as the person to whom permission to depart has been granted by the Secretary
of State, the applicant may be permitted to depart from the United States through the port
specified.
This application should be filed at least 30 days before the applicant intends to depart.
Any permit issued upon the basis of this application is subject to revocation at any time.
Every question must be answered in some manner, as no application will be acted upon
until complete information is furnished and unless it appears that the applicant has been
granted permission to enter the country of his destination and to pass through all countries
en route.
THE DEPARTURE CONTROL OFFICER WILL FILL IN THIS SPACE

for Mr. and Mrs. Pedro Rionda tc visit Japan were presented to the

iss Wolberg telephoned Mr. de Castro of the Philippine Embassy to

Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: September 26, 1947
SUBJECT:
Entry into Japan of Pedro Rionda a Philippine national
and his American wife.
PARTICIPANTS: Mr. Castro, Philippine Embassy
Mr. O'Brien, DS
PROTECTIVE SERVICES
COPIES TO:
SEP.261947
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
eDe
1-1408
Mr. Castro called Mr. Marx to ascertain the status
of the case of the above who desired to visit Japan en
route to the Philippines. Mr. Marx passed the case on
to Mr. O'Brien who cheoked with the military authorities.
Mr. O'Brien learned that the military authorities would
not process this case as the purpose of the visit was for
personal reasons. The military authorities stated a
memorandum in this connection would follow.
Mr. O'Brien called Mr. Castro and informed him of
the situation with respect to this case. Mr. Castro
seemed satisfied and stated he would press it no further
and indicated that he understood that the visit would be
in the nature of sightseeing. When Mr. O'Brien asked
him if he would like a memorandum concerning this case
Mr. Castro said no.

could take it to the French Embassy to have the required
notation put in as Sister Lucie has been cleared for
entry into Japan by SCAP.
Mr. Mohler informed Mr. O'Brien that Sister Lucie
had received her clearance through the military authorities
and was already on the high seas en route to Japan.
Therefore the case with respect to Sister Lucie may be
considered closed.

4 June 1947
Commercial airlines representatives here have suggested
that authority be delegated to American Consuls in large fore-
ign ports of call through which American flag carriers operate
or contemplate operation and to State Department representatives
in American cities on air routes to the Orient to validate
passports of American citizens for transit through Japan. Be-
lieved here that many last minute passengers would be unable
travel via American commercial aircraft if present lengthy
routine of obtaining JCS military permits for in-transit Japan
is retained. Believed here such delegation of authority would
be desirable in the interest of economy of administration since
it would eliminate much needless correspondence and many radios.
If no objection by you, request following instructions be passed
with least practicable delay to the Consuls and State Depart-
ment representatives listed.
1. Only clearances they may grant are for in-transit
travel through Japan. This contemplates no stay in Japan longer
than ordinary refueling stop or emergency stopover.
2. Individuals so cleared must clearly understand that
their conduct is controlled during any stop in Japan by the
agencies designated by the Supreme Commander for The Ailied

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.99)
Form AD-1
Alien Departure Permit
Application No.
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ALIEN'S APPLICATION FOR PERMISSION TO DEPART FROM
THE UNITED STATES
(For use by all aliens who desire to depart from the United States)
No fee prescribed
Send no money
This application must be executed in quintuplicate and sworn to béfore an officer
authorized to administer oaths. Each blank space must be filled in. If the space provided
is not sufficient to include the information requested, additional sheets of paper may be
utilized, with a reference to the question answered.
Five unmounted photographs of the applicant, recently made, not smaller than 2 by 2
nor larger than 3 by 3 inches, must be furnished with this application; one photograph to
be firmly affixed to each copy of the application in the blank space provided therefor. Each
photograph must be signed by the applicant in such manner as not to obscure the features.
An applicant registered or enrolled, or subject to registration or enrollment, for mili-
tary service in the United States must submit with this application a statement from the
Secretary of War or from his local draft board stating whether there are any objections to
his departure from the United States.
Other evidence may be required by the Secretary of State concerning the facts in the
applicant's case before a conclusion is reached on the application.
The receipt of the application will be acknowledged, and the applicant will be notified
later concerning the decision reached upon the application. If the permit to depart is
granted it will be forwarded to the appropriate authorities at the port through which the
applicant intends to depart from the United States, and the application number will appear
on a notification card which will be sent to the applicant. By calling upon the port author-
ities prior to or at the time of departure and presenting the notification card and identify-
ing himself as the person to whom permission to depart has been granted by the Secretary
of State, the applicant may be permitted to depart from the United States through the port
specified.
This application should be filed at least 30 days before the applicant intends to depart.
Any permit issued upon the basis of this application is subject to revocation at any time.
Every question must be answered in some manner, as no application will be acted upon
until complete information is furnished and unless it appears that the applicant has been
granted permission to enter the country of his destination and to pass through all countries
en route.

endorsed uy the respective representative missionaries.

facilities are available).
The quota for Siam is two; therefore, it is suggested that the Siamese
CS/V

national, to secure a military permit to enter Japan. The
Department transmitted Mr. Abadi's application to the Civil

encugh information regarding his purposes in Japan, his
method of travel, etc. It 1s proposed to make SCAp's views
krown to the Panamanian Embassy by the underlying draft
note and to suggest that Mr. Abadi re-submit his application

WAR DEPARTMENT
WAR DEPARTMENT SPECIAL STAFF
WASHINGTON 25, D. C.
26 November 1947
DIVISION OF
PROTECTIVE SERVICES
NOV 61947
MEMORANDUM FOR MR. CHAPPELL:
REPENT OF STATE
Documentation for intransit travel through Japan
of civil Airline passengers is no longer reguired. It is
now the responsibility of civil airline carriers concerned
to inform passengers transiting Japan that the passengers
are subject to all rules and regulations promulgated by the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers while in Japan for
normal refueling stop or stop for emergency weather condi-
tions or mechanical failure.
The civil airline concerned must also exercise
general responsibility for the conduct of its passengers
while the passengers are in Japan.
Pan American and Northwest Airlines will be noti-
fied of the above.
JOHN E. KEOUGH
Captain, GSC
Military Permit Section

imperative, if the I. T. T. companies re really to contribute to the
work done by the occupying forces, that experienced American personnel
be allowed to o to Japan to tate over the over-all management on a
permanent basis. Our two manufacturing subsidiaries in Japan are at

communication esuipment but they  re not producing this equipment as

WITKOWSKI NEW YORK AGENCY, Inc.
EXPORT—COMMISSION AGENTS-IMPORT
70
32O BROADWAY
NEW YORK 7. N. Y.
August 18, 1947
TO WHOM IT MAY CONCERN
With reference to the attached application for permission to visit Japan,
I would like to set forth the reasons for my application.
I was Managing Director of J. Witkowski & Co., Ltd, Japan, from 1935
until the outbreak of war. My firm, however, was established in Japan in 1887.
The firm in Japan was both interested in imports and exports and represented
the following companies:
Les Fils de P. Bardinet, Paris
Cadbury-Fry Export Department, Birmingham
California Packing Corporation, San Francisco
Colgate-Palmolive-Peet Co., Chicago
J. & J. Colman, Ltd., London
G. & L. Fli Cora, Torino
Adolph Frankau & Co., Ltd., London
John Haig & Co., Ltd., London
R. Johnston & Co., Bordeaux
Kellogg Sales Co., Battle Creek
Mestrezat-Preller, Bordeaux
Royal Baking Powder Co., New York
Yardley & Co., Ltd., London
Schwov & Freres, Tavannes
In addition to the above agencies, we imported commercial diamonds, textiles
and made up apparels in the better grades for Japanese consumtion. To the
best of my memory, our yearly imports were approximately one and a half
million yen.
Our main business in Japan was exports to all parts of the world. We used
to sell very considerably quantities of goods to America through our agents
Melvin W. Peyser, 320 Broadway, New York
Daniel J. Porter, 27 E. Monroe, Chicago
Getz Bros. & Co., 231 Sansome Street, San Francisco
and some of our clients were:
Montgomery Ward & Sears Roebuck in Chicago
Hub Floral Company, Boston
Chicago Mail Order Company, Chicago
Western Hosiery Company, Chicago
Caradine Hat Company, St. Louis
Men's Hats, Inc., Baltimore
Pan Pacific Importers, Los Angeles

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.98)
DC
-2-
We al o had a very large clientelle in England, France, Belgium, Scandinavia,
Australia, New Zealand, Africa and South America. Our yearly exports from Japan
were approximately ten to twelve million yen.
When the war broke out, the Japanese seized all our stocks in warehouses
and to this date we have not been able to ascertain our position, vis a vis
our losses in Japan. Whilst we have very heavy claims against the Japanese
Government, we are likewise indebted to the Hongkong & Shanghai Banking Corppration
in Japan.
Consequently, the purpose of my application to return to Japan is firsty,
to see the possibilities of reopening our offices to carry on the trade in
imports and exports that we had formerly; secondly to adjust our affairs with
our bankers and ascertain the prospects of recovery of the stocks confiscated
by the Japanese.
I think it only right to tell you that I am a British subject, having
entered here under the imnigration quota and Witkowski New York Agency, Inc.,
is owned by myself and Mr. Melvin W. Peyser, a citizen of the United States.
I did approach the Board of Trade in London, who advised me that inasmuch as
I have an office here in America, I should make my application through your
office.
I feel that it is the policy of the American Government to encourage
and foster trade with Japan and that in reopening our offices in Japan, we
could be one of the means of serving that purpose.
I can also tell you that I have received communications from all our
old clients the world over, regarding my plans for Japan and there is no
doubt that with my long knowledge of Japan, having resided there from 1912
until 1940, I would have full support from our clients abroad for both
imports and exports.
Needless to add, in the event of my being enabled to reopen my Japanese
offices, it would be in conjunction with the office that I now have in New York
City.
Very truly yours,
4/oc.l... a..
AG:LW
ALFRED GOLDMAN

STANDARD FORM NG. 64
CONFIDENTIAL
TO
NA, Mr. Allison
DATE: October 28, 1947
FROM
SUBJECT:
Telegram No. 277 from Tokyo October 22, 1947
With reference to a telephone conversation with Mr. Fearey
and the attached telegram from Tokyo No. 277 of October 22, 1947.
we have talked this over with Mr. Paige of OE, who points out
that a broadcast to Malaya explaining the regulations for business-
FW 894.
men's entry into Japan may run the risk of offending the British,
who for one reason or another may wish to limit the number of
Malay businessmen desiring to go to Japan. These reasons,
Mr. Paige says, include the overall limitation of UK businessmen
to a total of 64, the racial aspects of the situation, the necessity
of having to supply businessmen with dollars in order to complete
transactions.
It is suggested, therefore, before we consider further any
broadcast of proper entry procedure, that you might wish to ask
1/10-2247
the Consul General in Singapore to look into the situation
in Singapore and even consult with the British to determine
their attitude on the travel to Japan of local Asiatic business-
men. We might be in a better position to handle this, following
the receipt of the Consul General's report. The action telegram
is being returned herewith.
FU-894
CS
Attachment:
EK
DEC 10 1947
FILED
ADO:EXroll/jg
2

Dear Mr. Whitman:
Jin Fullam has just telephoned me from New York and notified
me of a change in the list of personnel which we wish to get into
Japan, which was submitted to you in Mr. Fullam's letter of October 20,
1947.
We wish to substitute Mr. E. N. Vendell as Chief Engineer in
place of C. A. Hayne. Mr. Wendell has been with the International Tele-
phone and Telegraph Corporation and associated companies for over twenty
years. He is a highly qualified radio engineer and until recently has
been Vice President in charge of the Federal Telephone and Radio Corpora-
tion, our manufacturing subsidiary in the United States.
I hope that this substitution will not cause you too much trouble.

CONTROL OF ENTRY AND EXIT OF IINDIVIDUALS,
AIRCRAFT AND SURF/ CE VESSLLS INTO AND FROM JÁPAN
Section
GENERAL.
INDIVIDUALS
.II
AIRCRAFT
.III
SURFACE VESSELS.
.IV
CUSTOMS AND PUBLIC HEALTH REGULATIONS..V
I. GENERAL. 1. Circular 3, General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, 8 June 1946, is
rescinded.
2. Purpose: Procedures to be followed in the control
of individuals, aircraft and surface vessels entering and
leaving Japan, i.e., the four (4) main islands of Hokkaido,
Honshu, Shikoku and Kyushu and outlying islands under
controi of the Cccupetion Forces.
3. Implementing Agencies:
a. The Conmanding General, Eighth Army, will
control the entry into and exit from Japan of the categories
of persons mentioned herein.
b. The Commander, Naval Activities Japan, will
control the entry and exit and the movements of surface
vessels while in Japanese waters.
c. The Commanding General, Pacific Air Command,
United States Army, will control the entry and exit and the
movements of aircreft into and out of Japan.
d. The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
will keep the implementing agencies informed as to the
action taken on requests for entry into and departure from
Japan of individuals, aircraft and surface vessels. This
infornr tion will include the period of velidity of the
permit ond area or areas in Japan to which individual can
travel.
e. The Commanding General, Eighth Army, will
estohlish passport, health and customs control at ports of
Entry and Exit, both air and surface, utilizing such
Japanese agencies as are appropriate. Such control will
include:
(1)
Medical examinations.
(2)
Giving of necessary inoculations.
(3)
Examination of baggage.
(4)
Issuance of permit of entry.
(5)
Maintenance of facilities for the
detention of unauthorized arrivals,
including food and shelter for air-
craft crews not permitted to leave
airports.
f. Implementing

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
f. Implementing agencies shall have power of
detention over individuals, aircraft and surface vessels
in the event of failure to comply with this circular.
4. Ports of entry for aircraft and surface vessels
will be designated by the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers, at the time of approval for entry. Currently, the
ports of entry for both aircraft end surface vessels are
included in the Tokyo-Yokohama erea.
5. The provisions of this circular do not apply to
persons being repatriated to and from Japan under the
sup rvision of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
II. INDIViDULLS. 6. Luthoriz-tion to Enter Japan:
a. Mombers of the Occupation Forces and individ-
uals required for service therewith, and their dependents
Will be authorized to enter Japen in accordrnce with
international ngreemcnts providing for the presence in
Jonn of such forces, subject to rules and regulations as
mey be established by the Supreme Commander for the llied
Powers.
b. Memburs of the Cccupotion Forces" are dcfined

governing the entry and exit of proporty and cargo of individuals entering

and Kyushu, and outlying islands under control of the occupation forces.

imports and exports into and from Japan. Suoh imports and exports must be
authorizod by an appropriato liconso validated by the Suprome Conmander for
the Alliod Powurs.

Japanese sconomy are prohibited from importing or exporting items into or
from Japan, oxcept whon specifically authorized by tho Supreme Commandor
for the lliod Powors or whon such itens are authorizod to bo transmitted
through intornational mail channols.

an automobilc, artiolos of porsonal adornmont, and othor offocts intended
and reasonably nocossary for tho porsonal uso of tho individual.

necessary for the use of an individual and his family in establishing or

aro intonded for and rcasonably nocossary to a normal individual vocation

Dear Mr. President,
I write in the hope you may help to overcome
the main obstacle to my early return to Japan, where I lived
for over nine years until repatriated to England under the
diplomatic excnange of Allied and Japanese nationals in 1942.
intil 'rearl larbour' I was associated with
a ritish merchant in Yokohana, r. .t. Jell, when I was ar-
rested uy Japanese police on a trumped-up charge of espionage
and detained in a iokohama gaol (in solitary confimement for
noout four months) for guestioning.
iving lost practically everything, with the
exception of ny ousiness experience and contacts in the Far
sast, I now desire to return to where I know my understanding
of the orient could be oi most service and at the same time
as ist the completion of my own recovery and renabilitation
which, I feel sure you can appreciate, means much to a man of
mid le age.

SUBJECT:
Procedure with reference to the cases of Japanese business-
men at Honolulu who have been approved by Commerce and
cleared by SCAP to enter Japan under the U.S. trader's quota.
I inquired of Mr. Spence of the Exit Permit Unit of
the Visa Division how the Visa Division formerly handled
cases such as the above since such cases apparently are
now going to be handled in DS.
Files
Central
RM/R 8
Mr. Spence informed me that the Visa Division after
it learns that SCAP has cleared the subject individuals
calls the Islands Possessions Division of the Department
of the Interior (Miss Hackett, dial 812, Ext. 4737) and informa0
it of all the facts of the clearance. The Islands Possessions 4
Division cables all these facts to the office of the Governor
of Hawaii, who in turn takes care of the necessary documenta-
tion.
It is proposed that Ds use the same procedure as
described ebove. Your comments are requested.

compliments to His Excellency the Secretary of State and has
the honor to inform him that the Private Secretary in the
Office of the President of Mexico has commissioned Mr. Robert
Gómez Cisneros to carry out a survey for the purpose of

permission of the competent authorities in order to be able
to travel to Japan and enter that country. In consequence,
it herewith encloses the pertinent application, in duplicate,
and respectfully requests the Department of State to be good
enough to take favorable action thereon.

Late in the afternoon of November 3, 1947, Mr. Ejel
called Mr. O'Brien to inform him that he encountered
difficulty in arranging transportation to Japan and
that he would not be able to utilize his clearance
valid between November 20 and December 5, 1947. Mr. Ejel
requested that a new clearance permit be granted him
for the period December 5 to December 20, 1947.
Mr. O'Brien informed Mr. Ejel that his request would
be communicated to the military authorities and that
CS/A
he would be informed.
Mr. O'Brien called Capt.Keough the morning of
November 4 and informed him of Mr. Ejel's request.
Captain Keough stated that Mr. Ejel's request could
be granted and that Mr. Ejel might be informed in this
regard. Captain Keough stated a memorandum of confir-
mation in this connection would be addressed to DS.
Mr. O'Brien, upon completion of his conversation
with Captain Keough, called Mr. Ejel and informed him
that his request was granted and advised him to commu-
nicate with the Syrian Legation at Washington so that
put in order because of the change.
FILES
Mr. OiBri

=== Page 422 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF COMMERCE
OFFICE OF INTERNATIONAL TRADE
EXPORT SUPPLY. BRANCH
Washington 25, D. C.
GENERAL LICENSE FOR THE EXPORT.TION OF PERSONAL BAGGAGE AND EFFECTS
1. Scope of License
a. A general license for personal baggage and effects, designated
BAGGAGE, authorizes the exportation of the following classes of commodities
by a person leaving the United States:
 Personal baggage.--Boxes, trunks, and other luggage containing items
of a personal nature, such as clothing, books, toilet articles, electric
razors, electric irons, articles of personal adornment, foodstuffs, soap,
medicinals, unexposed photographic film, cameras, firearms and ammunition,
-souvenirs, etc.
Personal effects.--(1) Household articles: furniture, refrigerators,
radios, decorations, and other household furnishings. (2) Professional
instruments and tools of trade: all instruments, tools, and apparatus
.which are used by the person in his profession or trade. (3) Vehicles:
passenger cars, station wagons, trucks, and trailers, and motorcycles.
b. Commodities may be exported under the general license for personal
baggage and effects only when they are for the exclusive use of the individual
leaving the United States or his immediate family. Articles intended for use
by other persons, or for resale, may not be exported under this general license.
The provisions of this general license are applicable to accompanied and un-
accompanied personal baggage, which are defined as follows:
Accompanied.--All commodities exported under this general license
on the same carrier on which the passenger departs.
Unaccompanied.--All other shipments of commodities under this
general license. Unaccompanied shipments under this category should
be clearly marked "Baggage."
C. Enforcement of the limitations imposed by this general license will
be by the Customs officials at the various ports of exits. They may limit or
prevent altogether the export of any commodity under this general license when-
ever, in their judgment, the amount is excessive or whenever they have reason
to believe that the exportation is made for the purpose of circumventing the
export regulations of the Office of International Trade, Department of Commerce.
2. Provisions for Specific Commodities.
a. Foods and Soaps.--The combined total domestic retail value of all
soap, butter, and other edible fats and oils must not exceed $5.00. The com-
bined total domestic retail value of all meat and meat products, soap, butter
and other edible fats and oils, must not exceed $50.00.
(over)

=== Page 423 === (confidence 0.98)
b. Firearms and ammunition--Exportations under this general license
are limited to three firearms and 50o rounds of ammunition per person.
SPECIAL PROVISIONS FOR MISSIONARIES
1.
1. The limitations on foods, soaps, butter and other dible fats and oils
which individuals may take out of the United States under the general license
for personal baggage and effects do not apply to missionaries with proper
credentials, provided they are to be out of the United States for more than
one year.
2. Collectors of Customs are authorized to release all food, soap, butter
and other edible fats and oils under general license as personal baggage for
missionaries subject to the following limitations:
a.
The total domestic retail value of butter must not exceed $5.00;
b.
The combined total domestic retail value of all soap, butter and
other edible fats and oils must not exceed $35.00; and
5.
The combined total domestic retail value of meat products, soap,
butter, and other edible fats and oiis must not exceed $150.00.
10/24/47

MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
Mr. Alfred Goldman, who is cleared by SCAP to enter
Japan between November 16 and November 30, 1947 on the
Trader's Quota, visited Mr. Chappell's office (this date)
to discuss his clearance with Mr. Chappell.
-1047
Mr. Goldman informed Mr. Chappell that he definitely
is interested in going to Japan but wishes to have a clear-
ance for a longer period of time than his present permit
will allow; in fact, he is interested in putting his busi-
ness on a permanent basis in Japan. Mr. Goldman requested
that his clearance be suspended temporarily until he could
determine whether he might enter Japan for a longer period
of time. If he could not be allowed to enter Japan on a
long time basis, Mr. Goldman stated he would like to utilize
his clearance at a later date and requested that his case
not be considered closed. Mr. Goldman stated he would
inform DS when he would be in a position to travel to Japan.

MEMORANDUM FOR MR. CHAPPELL:
Reference is made to your memorandum of 31 October
1947, concerning the travel of Emilio P. Virata to Japan.
Theater Headquarters has informed this office that
1-1347
in view of the fact that the Philippine Grand Lodge has never

Department of Commerce

Gentlemen:-
Please be advised that our imports from Japan
in the years 1936 to 1941 were made in the name of our
parent company---General Import Co..
We have since formed a corporation under the
name of Genic Export-Import Co.Inc.and our importing and

STANDARD FORM NO. 64
TO
A-A
DATE: November 19, 1947
THROUGH: SS/CR
FROM
ACON - Mr. Robinson
suBJEct: Communication to the Panamanian Embassy at Washington of 
clearance by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers at
Tokyo for two Panamanian nationals, Mr. Eduardo and Mr.Isidoro
Azrak, to enter Japan.
894.1
DISCUSSION:
The Department received two applications submitted
separately by the Panamanian Embassy at Washington under
cover of 1ts respective notes of August 13, 1947 and July 7,
1947 for Messrs. Eduardo and Isidoro Azrak to enter Japan.
The Department transmitted these applications along with
what information was available in the Department's files con-
cerning the subject gentlemen to the Military Permit Office
1747
of the Department of the Army to be communicated by that
office to the Supreme Commander for approval or disapproval.
In a memorandum dated November 17. 1947 the Military Permit
Office informed the Department that the Supreme Commander
approves the entry into Japan of Messrs. Azrak. It 1s
proposed to communicate the facts of these clearances to
the Panamanian Embassy at Washington by the underlying draft
note.
RECOMMENDATION:
It 1s recommended that the underlying draft note be
signed.

=== Page 450 === (confidence 0.98)
DClR
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
November 20, 1947
In order to be absolutely certain that all the
information, much of it adverse, in connection with
the case of the Azrak brothers had been transmitted
by the Department of the Army to SCAP, Mr. Chappell
called Captain Keough to check further on this mat-
ter. Captain Keough informed Mr. Chappell that all
the information supplied to his office by the Depart-
ment of State in connection with the Azraks was made
available to SCAP and presumably was considerea when
approval was given for the issuance of military entry
permits. However, since Mr. Chappell had raised the
question of the security factor involved in the issu-
ance of military permits to these men, Captain Keough
again checked with his superior, Colonel Lynch, who
decided that all the information in the case had been
made available to the military authorities in Japan
and that no further action would be taken by the
Department of the Army.

MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
894
November 25, 1947
A letter dated September 28, 1947 from Mr. P. H.
Andrews, a British national, was received in DS Octo-
ber 10, 1947. In this letter Mr. Andrews requested
permission for him and his wife to disembark at Yoko-
hama while the vessel on which they were travelling
to Singapore was in port at Yokohama. Mr. Andrews
stated that he was travelling aboard the SS Marine
-2547
Swallow sailing from San Francisco October 10, 1947,
the same date that his letter was received by DS. In
view of the time element it is apparent that Ds could
not communicate with Mr. Andrews concerning his re-
quest (which would have to be denied since SCAp does
not pass favorably on such requests) before his depar-
ture date.
A check with American President Lines, owners of
the SS Marine Swallow, revealed that Mr. and Mrs. Andrews
sailed For Singapore from San Francisco, October 10,
1947, as they proposed.

I. GENERAL. 1. Circular 3, General Hendquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, & June 1946, is
rescinded.
2. Purpose: Procedures to be followed in the control
of individuals, aircraft ond surface vessels entering and
leaving Japan, i.e., the four (4) main islands of Hokkaido,
Honshu, Shikoku end Kyushu end outlying islands under

3. Implementing Agencies:
a. The Commanding General, Eighth Army, will
control the entry into and exit from Japan of the categories
of persons mentioned herein.
b. The Commander, Naval Activities Japan, will
control the entry and exit and the movements of surface
vessels while in Japanese waters.
c. The Commanding General, Pacific Air Command,
United States Army, will control the entry and exit nd the
movements of airereft into ond out of Japen.

action token on requests for entry into and departure from

permit and area or areas in Jopan to which individual can
travel.
c. The Commanding General, Eighth Army, will
estahlish passport, henlth and customs control at ports of
Entry and Exit, both air and surfece, utilizing such
Japanese agencies as are approprirte. Such control will
include:
(1)

(2)

(3)

(4)
Issuance of permit of entry.
(5)
Maintenance of facilities for the
detention of unauthorized arrivals,
including food and shelter for air-
craft crews not permitted to leave
Pirports.

included in the Tokyo-Yokohama area.

persons being repatriated to and from Japan under the

Mr. O'Brien telephoned Mr. Bennett to inform him that
a military permit indicating that Mr. Abadi was cleared by
SCAP for entry into Japan on the Trader's Cuota between
January 1 and January 15, 1948 was received by DS this date.
Mr. Abadi's stay in Japan is limited to twenty-one days be-
ginning the date of entry and he should take with him to
Japan two standard photographs measuring 1 and 1/4 inches
by 1 and 3/4 inches. Mr. OiBrien requested that Mr. Bennett
communicate this information by telephone to Mr. Chevalier,
Minister Counselor of the Panamanian Embassy, and invite Mr.
Chevalier's attention to the appropriate paragraphs of SCAP
Circular 8 of August 30, 1946 so that the Panamanian Embassy
or Foreign Office could document Mr. Abadi appropriately for
his travel.
Mr. Bennett agreed to Mr. O'Brien's request and asked
Mr. O'Brien to write a 3d person note to the Panamanian
Embassy confirming his telephone call.

In reply to your letter of the 5th inst. addressed to
Mr. Isaak Yanowitch, Vice President of our firm, we
list below the information requested, viz;
1- Mr. Isaak Yanowitch holds Passport No. CH 75/46
issued by the Polish Consulate in Shanghai, China
on August 3th,1946 and extended by the Polish
Consul in Mexico City until Sept. 15th,1949.
12-947
This passport has been visaed by the U.S. Consul
in Mexico City on October 23rd,1947, #7549 permitting
him to travel to and from the United States for a
period of one year and for an unlimited number of
untrys and departures during that period.
2- Mr. Isaak Yanowitch plans to depart on December 25th,1947.
3- Mr. Isaak Yanowitch will depart from San Franciscom Cal.
via Pan-American Airways.
Inasmuch as Mr. Yanowitch is still detained in Mexico City and
will be there for some time yet and whatever available time he
will have in New York will have to be spent in the interests of
our firm here, we would appreciate very much if you could send
his permit for entry into Japan to our office so that no time
we be lost. However, if it is absolutely necessary for hin to

=== Page 546 === (confidence 0.99)
November 28, 1947
Mr. Gilbert Fcrd
Department of Commerce
Empire State Building
New York City
Dear Sir:
We want to express our very best thanks for. your having ad-
vised us that our company had been granted authorization to
send a representative to visit Japan. We understand, how-
ever, that the name of Mr. Frank Francis has not yet been
approved and in any case as we advised you over the phone,
Mr. Frank Francis, whom we had to send to Manila on an ur-
gent matter, just left the Philippines three days ago re-
turning to San Francisco.
Our plans were to consolidate his trip to Manila with a visit
to Japan, but now inasmuch as the necessary authorization
could not be secured as rapidly as was needed, barring unfore-
seen circumstarces we shall have to postpone our visit to
Japan and will very probably not take advantage of the au-
thorization granted fo the latter part of December.
We shall certainly comnunicate with you as soon as our repre-
sentative will be in a position to enter Japan, and now that
our company's name has been put on the approved list, we hope
that arrangenents can be completed more rapidly in the future.
Thanking you again for all your efforts and for your kind
attention, ve remain
Very truly yours,
A. SCHULMAN INC.
P. Baudry
